Narrator:Lone Star's dock ring hits you with coolant stink, fried grease, and the low, constant thrum of a station bracing for war; your bruises are old already, but the silence where Black Trace used to be is still fresh. Asha learns the ship market's a joke unless you've got military keys. Maris calmly informs her you're rich now and asks, half laughing and half horrified, why you want a battleship, and Kaelith comes back from his digging with worse news: the armor and that freighter are more than Inuran salvage. They are Nefarius god-blood infused weapons of an old war. Over drinks, grief turns into vows. The Catalyst map comes out, then the Augury, your faces, the dock's unmaking, and the unmistakable feeling of an unseen eye calmly watching you reach for Aashitak like it already knew you would. War news floods in regarding Virkon and Xal, and then Major Seraphine Morrow drops the offer like a hammer: rendezvous in ten hours, take a ship, join the Confederacy and see the war through. 311. Narrator:You follow Seraphine's map through the station’s military side and the air changes. There is much less fryer grease, more ozone and disinfectant, like the corridor itself got promoted. Marines in hard boots move in lanes, not clusters; every bulkhead has a muster board, every third door has a guard with a bored stare and a live weapon.
312. 
313. Ahead, in a private dock cradle big enough to feel like a hangar for a city, **_Morrow’s Law_** sits nose‑down toward the void like it’s already impatient. The hull is the same brutal Confederacy slab shape you’d expect from a Ternus Battleship, **dorsal spine raised like a broken knife**, ventral heat trenches carved deep. This one’s pristine, plates fitted tight and edges sharp, paint not scuffed into humility yet. It's not a young ship however. This one has recently been completely refit.

389. Narrator:The gangway is open. Marines are moving crates and ammo along marked lanes. A pair of spellfighters are being towed into alignment like sleeping sharks.
390. 
391. Boots hit the gangway and the sound changes. Hollow metal, then deeper plating, then the subtle vibration of a ship that’s awake. The air at the threshold is cooler and cleaner than the dock, laced with coolant and gun oil. A launch countdown ticks on a bulkhead display in calm red numbers.
392. 
393. Inside, it’s controlled chaos. Squads staging, deck crews shouting short numbers, someone sealing a crate with a hiss of polymer and a prayer. You pass a hatch where marines are checking breacher charges. You pass another where a mage in uniform is arguing quietly with an engineer about the drive.
394. 
395. Nobody slows down to stare at the group.
396. 
397. The inner hatch seals behind you with a heavy, final clunk, and the station noise drops away like you just stepped underwater. Ahead are bright corridor lights, arrows painted on the deck, and the strong sense that you could get lost on this ship.
398. 
399. A man in an impeccable uniform and a full buzz cut walks toward you at top speed, moving with absolutely zero sense of wasted time. "Captain Asha Morrow?"
400. 
401. Asha Morrow:"Yes."

403. Sergeant Smoke:He stops just five feet away and firmly salutes. "Sergeant Smoke reporting for duty, sir. The Major is waiting for you on the bridge. Do you need to drop anything off or can we head straight there?"
404.

409. Sergeant Smoke:Sergeant Smoke pivots on his heel and you move. He does not “lead” so much as he sets a pace that assumes you can keep up. The corridors tighten as you climb. The deck under your boots goes from cargo-grade thrum to citadel-grade steadiness. Fewer pallets. More sealed doors. More cameras that do not pretend to be polite.
410. 
411. Every few steps, you pass marines in partial kit with checklists on holo slates. Every few minutes, you pass the same kind of quiet intensity in different uniforms. Engineers with grease on their knuckles. Mages with tired eyes and stained gloves. Officers whose faces say they have not slept and are proud of it.
412. 
413. A klaxon chirps once. Not an alarm. A reminder. Someone’s voice on the intercom calls out a time hack. Launch window shrinking.
414. 
415.

417. Narrator:The bridge hatch is a heavy iris door with layered seals and arcane symbols of warding magitech etched into the metal like a scar. Smoke palms the access plate, the wards breathe, and the door opens with a soft pressure change that makes your ears pop.
418. 
419. The bridge is not wide. It is deep.
420. 
421. A raised command dais sits forward, wrapped by a crescent of stations. The holo tactical table hangs in the center, layered with fleet icons, range rings, and lines of probability that keep updating faster than your eyes want to track. The lighting is purposeful. Bright over the boards, dim over everything else. You can see faces without seeing comfort.
422. 
423. To port, gunnery stations are stacked in tiers, each with hardline controls for missiles and kinetic weaponry. To starboard, sensors and electronic warfare are all clean angles and quiet stations, screens showing passive sweeps and resonance readouts that flicker in bands your brain tries to interpret as meaning.
424. 
425. Behind the command dais, three gorgeous, universal spell matrices. They are state of the art, designed with bracing rings and made so that a collapsing mage has almost no chance of accidentally clipping one of the rotating rings, which automatically adjust themselves on the fly.
426. 
427. Major Seraphine Morrow:Major Seraphine Morrow is waiting at the forward edge of the command dais, hands clasped behind her back, eyes on the holo. She turns when you enter.
428. 
429. Her uniform is immaculate in the way only a person who refuses to be tired can manage. The cut is sharp. The insignia is clean. There is a faint dust smear on one shoulder that she has not brushed off, which is how you know she has been somewhere real for the past few hours, not just in briefings. Her hair is pulled back tight. Her posture reads like a capital ship at rest.
430. 
431. Narrator: Asha:This hits like stepping back into gravity. You know that look. You grew up under it. It is love, filtered through duty until it becomes usable.
432.

491. Narrator:Smoke waits until Seraphine’s attention snaps back to the holo and the bridge rhythm swallows her again. Then he gestures, crisp and efficient, and leads you out through a side hatch that keeps you clear of the command lanes.
492. 
493. Two corridors down, the ship changes flavor. Still tight. Still clean. But the air is warmer and the lighting is softer, like someone remembered humans have to sleep to keep bleeding.
494. 
495. Smoke stops at a cluster of doors arranged around a small common nook. A fold-out table. A water unit. A wall screen already showing departure status and casualty projections that someone has thoughtfully muted.
496. 
497. Sergeant Smoke:“Assigned quarters,” Smoke says. “These are not luxury, but they’re yours. Thirty minutes until undock. Ten until everyone needs to be strapped in or useful.”
498. 
499. 
500. 
501. Narrator:For Asha, it is a Confederacy officer cabin stripped down to function. A bunk built into the wall with a restraint net already clipped and ready. A tiny desk with hardline ports and a locked drawer. A narrow locker rated for armor and weapons, with a mag-seal that gives off the faintest hum when you get close.
502. 
503. For Kaelith and the others: Same bones, slightly different fit. The locker is bigger, reinforced at the corners, and there is a small ward-etched strip along the frame that quietly says the ship expects mages to do stupid things when tired.
504. 
505. Rho and Tamsin get the functional bunks across the nook, with a shared weapons rack that looks like it has seen arguments settled quickly. Lysa’s cabin is adjacent, close enough that she can be on the corridor in seconds, far enough that she can shut a door when she needs to think.
506.

597. Narrator:Everyone straps in when the alarm sounds and the Morrow's Law departs. Ten minutes later it is full burn toward the Umbral Shadow of the planet. And then it slips into the Depths. The ship goes dark and quiet, interior lighting unaffected but the hum of the vessel is now soft. The crew is overall muted and somber at this point if any of them pass by. They don't bother you.
598. 
599. In just twenty minutes you are back in real space again. Morrow transfers you to the new ship at a station in space hung far out in the darkness beyond a gas giant in a random system. The station is purely for war supplies and ship docking.
600. 
601. The crew is now transferred now to the "Lone Star", a class of Battleship modeled similar to Seraphine's ship. Everyone makes their way to the bridge and takes up their stations. The orders are to follow a very clear and rapid set of routes through the Depths.
602. 
603. You will emerge from them in a warzone. The strategy is for you to appear just out from the planet's shadow as the main fleet has already taken the fight and has Virkon's forces distracted. You are to get into position and likely pull off a key target or two from them. Take those targets out, and try to regroup with the main fleet if possible.
604. 
605. The bridge itself is a long, layered wedge. Forward is glass and armor and sensor geometry. Aft is bulkheads and doors that can seal like fists. The command dais sits raised, with stations arranged in a crescent so nobody has to turn their back on the holo. The tactical table hangs in the middle, painting the war in clean shapes. Range rings. Threat cones. Lines of drift and probable intercepts. It updates too fast to be polite.
606. 
607. Through the forward panels you catch the dock cradle framing the outside world. The battleship’s bow reads like a cathedral built for instruments. Plates step inward around a forward aperture array. Passive stacks sit in dark, faceted blocks. The whole prow looks like it was designed to listen for a god breathing.
608. 
609. 
610. 
611. Narrator:Below all of that, you can feel the ship’s spine. That raised dorsal ridge is not decorative. It is a focusing rail. A long, straight promise that the ship can take whatever spell you pour into it and turn it into something with range and consequences.
612.

631. Narrator:The trip is a rollercoaster of jaunts in and out of the Depths. Five short jumps in total, all in under thirty minutes. When you emerge from the other side, it is in the shadow of Virkon. The planet is a rocky expanse with thin excuses for oceans and a sparse amount of green life.
632. 
633. Tamsin immediately directs the scry screen to the battle going on at a small asteroid belt not far out from Virkon. The Confederate fleet of two hundred destroyers, fifty cruisers, and ten battleships are all weaving their way quickly through the asteroids. Outriders, elite War Mages, chase them on the backs of massive dragons. The size of the dragons makes navigating the asteroids difficult.
634. 
635. Just as you are watching a couple of the destroyers get picked off by bolts of electricity ripping out of the jaws of two wyrms, suddenly a silent wreath of explosions wraps their bodies. Micro mines full of nanites detonate and simultaneously destroy the bodies of the wyrms, turning them to shreds and dust. The sight is devastatingly powerful. Ternus has never deployed these outside of orbital defenses. To see them in the field is breathtaking in their simplicity. Too small to scan, but instantly deadly to ship or wyrm alike.

## Session 7 Summary

**For You (GM Prep):**

Session 7 was a massive escalation. The crew completed their decision to join the Confederacy's war against Virkon. They met Major Seraphine Morrow aboard her battleship *Morrow's Law*, received their own battleship (*Lone Star*), and deployed to the warzone. Key developments:

1. **Sargoth Awakens**: Kaelith's Nefarius god-blood armor has catalyzed into a full Eldimagus—a living, sentient artifact. It named itself Sargoth and hungers for magic. It recognized Virkonna as "familiar." This is a ticking time bomb narratively.

2. **Ship Combat Success**: The crew worked smoothly in their first real ship combat, taking down an Elder Wyrm with a disintegration bolt and gauss cannon combo. Asha piloted, Kaelith ran offense, Lysa handled wards/counterspells, Tamsin provided targeting, and Rho fired conventional weapons.

3. **Total Fleet Annihilation**: Virkonna herself awoke and obliterated the Ternus fleet in seconds—four battleships shredded instantly, the rest engulfed in her breath. Seraphine's ship went down with the others.

4. **Xal Aran Intervenes**: The Guardian of Xal arrived in his dreadnought *The Word of Xal*, a literal god-killer. He prevented Virkonna from destroying the cloaked *Lone Star* and demanded the crew dock with him. Virkonna detected the "smell" of Nefarius—almost certainly Sargoth—and nearly attacked before Aran stopped her.

5. **Seraphine Lives?**: Aran cryptically assured Asha that her mother "will be waiting for her" aboard the dreadnought. How and why remains unexplained.

**For Next Session**: The crew is about to board the *Word of Xal* and meet Xal Aran face-to-face. Virkonna knows Nefarius-tainted artifacts are present. The armor's nature may come under scrutiny. Seraphine's survival needs explanation. The players are now entangled in god-tier politics between Xal and Virkonna—no longer pawns, but pieces on a much larger board.

---

## Player Recap (In Narrator Voice)

**Narrator:**
You joined a war. You killed a dragon. Then a god woke up and ate your fleet. The *Lone Star* slipped through Virkonna's wrath only because you were invisible—until Xal Aran's revelation sweep lit you up like a signal flare between two titans. Virkonna smelled something on your ship, something old, something she *remembered*. She wanted to burn you for it, and she would have, if the Guardian hadn't aimed a god-killer at her throat. Now you're docking with a dreadnought the size of a city, your mother's fate uncertain, your armor whispering in hunger, and the two most dangerous beings in the known universe have both seen your face. Welcome to the war. You wanted answers? They're coming—whether you're ready or not.

---

## Session 7 Summary (GM Prep)

---

### What Happened

The crew committed to the Confederacy. Seraphine transferred them to the *Lone Star*, a battleship with three universal spell matrices. Kaelith's Nefarius god-blood armor catalyzed into a sentient Eldimagus—named itself **Sargoth**, hungers for magic, whispered "*War*" with pleasure. They deployed to Virkon.

Ship combat went clean. Asha piloted, Kaelith fired disintegration bolts, Lysa warded, Tamsin locked targets, Rho ran the gauss cannon. One Elder Wyrm down. Fleet pushed toward the planet.

Then Virkonna woke.

The Goddess shredded four battleships instantly, breath attack wiped the rest. Seraphine's ship went down. The *Lone Star* survived only because of the stealth coating. Then Xal Aran's dreadnought *The Word of Xal* blinked in, revealed them with a sweep spell, and stopped Virkonna from incinerating the crew by threatening mutually assured destruction. Virkonna smelled Nefarius—almost certainly Sargoth. Aran ordered the crew to dock.

Aran told Asha her mother would be "waiting for her" aboard the dreadnought. How and why: unexplained.

---

### Threads Open for Session 8

| Thread | Notes |
|--------|-------|
| **Sargoth's nature** | Virkonna recognized Nefarius. Aran's reveal spell lit them up. The armor is a liability now. |
| **Seraphine alive?** | Aran implied she survived. Rescue? Teleportation? Something else? |
| **Xal Aran meeting** | The crew is about to board a god-killer dreadnought and meet the Guardian of Xal face-to-face. |
| **Virkonna's paranoia** | She believes Inurans are rearming Ternus with old weapons. She's not wrong. |
| **Gods-tier politics** | Asha and Kaelith are no longer pawns. They're pieces on a board between Xal and Virkonna. |

---

## Read-Aloud (Primary) — Player Recap

**Narrator:**

You joined a war. You killed a dragon. Then a god woke up and ate your fleet.

The *Lone Star* slipped through Virkonna's wrath only because you were invisible—until Xal Aran's revelation sweep lit you up like a signal flare between two titans. Virkonna smelled something on your ship, something old, something she *remembered*. She wanted to burn you for it, and she would have, if the Guardian hadn't aimed a god-killer at her throat.

Now you're docking with a dreadnought the size of a city. Your mother's fate is uncertain. Your armor whispers in hunger. The two most dangerous beings in the known universe have both seen your face.

Welcome to the war. You wanted answers? They're coming—whether you're ready or not.

---

**Group Choice: How do you approach docking with the *Word of Xal*?**

1. **Comply quietly** — dock, disarm, wait for escort.
2. **Demand answers first** — open comms, ask about Seraphine before you land.
3. **Prepare for the worst** — dock but stay armed, ready to fight or run.

<sub>⏱️ 4m 46s</sub>

### 👤 User

Read through all session logs and evaluate them for EXP allotment: In the real world people improve at the things they
practice, and the same is true in The Magitech
Chronicles . At the end of each game session the
game master will award between 2 - 5 experience to
each player. Players may spend that experience to
improve their skills, attributes, and defenses. They
may also purchase spells or miracles if their path
grants access.
Completing a session is generally worth 2 XP. If you
showed up and played that’s the bare minimum a
character will walk out with.
However, roleplaying is meant to be a shared story,
and the Magitech Chronicles is designed to facilitate that. Experience is awarded when a player does some-
thing to aid the story. Here are some common reasons
to award a point beyond the initial 2:

AWARDING EXPERIENCE
• Played in character convincingly
• Accomplished a major story goal
• Made the other players laugh with an in character
action
• Suggested a plan or action that really made a
difference
• Encouraged someone else in the group in cha-
racter or out
As a GM you can add or remove reasons, but you
want a simple system to award xp. In general I try to
keep XP totals similar, so at the end of a session most
players will get 4 XP, and one might get 5. If someone
was really distracted, or detracting from the session,
they might only get 3

44. Tessa “Spoke” Arden is a lean, grease-fingered pilot with a habit of counting maneuver timings under her breath; she favors clean entries, catalogued hull condition, and keeping resale value intact. Her brother Joren is broader, burn-scarred, and impatient. He sits, twirling a plasma cutter like a baton and pushing for fast action over finesse.
45. 
46. Tessa Arden is lean and angular, built like the ship's control yoke she rarely lets go of. Close cropped dark hair shows a faint graphite sheen under the cockpit lights. A narrow, triangular scar sits at her temple where a headset once tore skin. Her flight harness is a worn copper-tan rig layered with sewn patch straps, scuffed at hip and shoulder from countless side-load dockings. Grease-black smudges stain the pads of her fingers and she wipes them absently on a gray cloth tucked beneath her collar before touching an antique panel. Her stance says held tension and controlled restraint.
47. 
48. Joren Arden stands half-turned toward the scry screen, broader through the chest and shoulders, posture casual like he's leaning into a bar conversation with danger. Burn-scar tracer lines, thin amber veins, web his forearms, disappearing under heat-scarred gloves. His hair is a tousled dark nest, a few strands singed lighter near the ends. His boots are heavier salvage pattern with the carbon toes scuffed white from age. Where Tessa is tight focus, Joren is kinetic readiness.
49.

55. 
56. -----
57. 
58. Your megablock apartment is a compact refuge. Filtered air rushes through the intake above a basic door. A neat, well-used bed sits opposite a muted holoscreen cycling trade news in translucent slate. A kettle's residual warmth clings to the small stove. A new encrypted contract pane blossoms on the holoscreen, six crescent glyph seals rotating slowly as a figureless voice speaks:
59. 
60. "Captain Asha. An Inuran war-era freighter drifts in your belt. I require intact tow custody before commercial phase begins. Ten standard hauls' payment. Timestamp acceptance grants temporary legal rights for you over the hull, permitting it to clear any docking permits. Will you accept?"
61. 
62. The glyphs lock and a set of light glass chimes fade into the deep structural thrum of the block. A narrow battery bar shows five green segments. A thin timestamp line crawls forward. The last wisp of steam vanishes and the air cools slightly. Distant elevator vibration can be felt among the groan of the city. Muffled steps can be heard out in the hallway.
63. 
64. Asha Morrow:Asha is a tall human captain built like a military officer that takes care of herself meticulously. Her fine black hair goes down to her shoulder with the right side shaved close. Asha's face is stern and her eyes focused, deep in thought as the holoscreen plays the message. She accepts the contract immediately and prepares to leave, grabbing her go-bag. Asha rushes through the apartment, turning everything off and heading out and soon as she is ready, suddenly feeling a deep yearning to get out into space.
65. 
66. Narrator: Asha:The contract accepts with a soft chime. A nav packet arrives on her device or with a clean vector to the contracted ship. It shows that is is parked in the asteroid belt between Ternus and the next planet out in the solar system.
67. 
68. A short message follows from the merchant, brief and polite. "Thank you, Captain. Intact tow preferred. Payment on delivery to the trade moon dock. Please store it at Ternus until negotiations are completed. I expect no more than a month."
69. 
70. Just like that, the bite of not negotiating the contract lands. Sure, it will pay out handsomely, but now she is going to be on the hook for all of the docking fees associated with keeping a whole other ship on dock along with her own. Either her savings will take a hit, or she will need to find something to 'salvage' from the wreck she finds before towing it.
71. 
72. Your hangar is quiet. Work lights stripe the floor in pale bars. A thin haze of coolant scent and warm dust meets you at the ramp. Your ship waits with belly lights on. Pre-flight comes back clean. Fuel and tow lashings are ready. The nav insert pings once more. Interference windows in the belt blink on your HUD like soft beats in a calm pulse as you examine a long range scan of the target location. It should be a straight shot, but not to anywhere that anyone would normally look. The ship seems to have been parked, or crashed into the dark side of a non-rotating asteroid among a strange cluster of many similarly sized rocks. None of them spin, the only thing setting them apart, but not strange enough to attract roaming eyes.
73. 
74. Asha Morrow:Asha exhales a heavy breath through her nose upon reading the acceptance message and starts considering all the uses she could have for a freighter to avoid a month of fees. Her face hardens as she mulls it over, going through pre-flight setup instinctually. Asha wastes no time and lifts off as soon as she is ready, following the provided route towards the asteroid. She examines the layout of the asteroids to familiarize herself with the destination and considers the lack of rotation on the asteroids a bad sign.
75.

110. Narrator: Asha:Dock control clears you to depart. You lift on a clean thrust and feel the ship settle into a steady climb. Lines on the HUD turn green in sequence. The bay door opens to cold stars. You nose out without drift or yaw. Traffic spacing is perfect. Your hands move through checks by habit. Engines answer with a smooth, even tone. With this takeoff you waste no time.
111. 
112. The vector loads and the course line reaches for the belt. Interference windows blink on your display like slow heartbeats. You time your burns to the gaps between asteroids. The ship slides into each window with light touches on the stick. The cabin is quiet apart from the hum of the drive and a soft tick from the panel when a pulse passes. Long‑range shows the target cluster as a dim knot. No beacon pings. No obvious patrol paths. It feels like a place people do not look.
113. 
114. You cross the boundary of the belt and match its plane. Rock slips by in slow frames. The cluster grows and resolves as a group of same‑sized bodies. None of them turn. The dark faces look carved from the same thought. Your HUD paints a careful arc around them and marks a safe lane. You trim speed and come in on a shallow approach. Not to your surprise since this is a stealth ship but there are absolutely no signs of a ship anywhere in the asteroid cluster. Not on visual scan, not on radar pings, no electromagnetic signals, nothing. Whatever the ship is, it sits on the dark side of the largest rock. In less than five minutes your braking burn will finish and you will be able to pull slowly around the rock and take a look for yourself at the freighter.
115. 
116. You have a little bit of time to prepare any gear or to announce your presence and intent. To anyone watching from a distance they will know not to interfere if you declare ownership over the vessel and broadcast the permits received from your contract.

118. Or you can always stay silent and choose to deal with that on an as-needed basis.

322. Narrator:The freighter is long and low. About one hundred ten meters from nose to tail and a little over twenty at its widest. The matte-black hull is smooth. Edges are beveled and every surface is broken by shallow facets that soak up light. Most markings are gone. Only faint Inuran stencil bands remain near recessed hardpoints. The skin is a sensor killing coat. It looks like a shadow in a field of shadows.
323. 
324. Inside the airlock the metal is dry and frost dull. The inner door is stiff but workable now that the ring is open. Emergency stripes run along the deck and walls in faded gold. Inuran glyph numbers mark sections at each bulkhead. The air is stale and thin. Your suit reads low oxygen and high carbon dioxide. The gravity is near zero. Your boots magnetize when they meet deck plates. Handholds line the corridor in neat rows.
325. 
326. The first interior corridor runs fore and aft. To the fore sits crew space and the bridge. You can expect a mess, bunks, a small wash, and a locker row. To the aft sits cargo and engineering. The armory is aft starboard behind a reinforced door just before the engineering ring. There are two main cargo bays midship

It spans the corridor from deck to overhead, a slab of dark alloy with three recessed locking bars set into the frame. The seam is tight and clean. Your boots click to the deck plate when you stop in front of it. Frost dusts the lower hinge and the handle of a manual crank that folds flat against the right side.

365. 
366. Its mark looks like a closed eye with three short lashes and a faint star where the pupil would be. The glow is cool and even. The other two sigils are dead. Their channels are cut deep and filled with dull metal. One is a tight, angular shape that steps inward like a broken spiral. The other is a smooth knot that loops back on itself in a figure eight. You do not recognize either of them.

462. Narrator:You feed a careful thread of power into the dead marks. The angular broken spiral drinks the charge and settles into a deep, matte black that seems to pull light inward. The smooth looping knot warms to a steady ember red. Dream stays cool and pale. The status strip slides from left to right in one clean pass. Inside the frame, three locking bars withdraw with a heavy clunk. Frost cracks along the lower hinge. No pressure push follows. The seam parts without a hiss. The door slides back on stiff rails and reveals a dark room lined in faded gold stripes.
463. 
464. Your suit lamps catch the shapes first. Two Inuran Mark V spell armor suits stand on wall hooks, black plates with faint iridescent veins and compact thruster vanes at the shoulders and hips.
465. 
466. A low, sealed locker sits at arm height. The latch gives with a firm pull. Inside are two Inuran Mark III spellpistols. They look grown more than machined, with sleek organic curves of obsidian alloy veined with faint violet motes that brighten when you grip them. The spiral focusing helix at each muzzle throws a tight corkscrew of light when fired. A soft harmonic tone rides the shot for the wielder alone. Each receiver has a micro‑glyph cluster ready to sync with a linked mount if you fit one on a suit.
467. 
468. On the lower shelf sits a vacuum pouch with five Stims. They are clear ampoules with fine silver particulate. They are injectable medicine with painkillers and healing nano‑bots. To the side, a strapped crate holds fifty Ternus MRE bricks.
469.

520. Whoever hired you they are either government or extremely well connected. How they knew it was here in the first place is a mystery. And you managed to get to it just before it was stolen or worse. The timing is too convenient.

526. Narrator:The whole job is as weird as you might expect for an Inuran. The trade moon meant to come into this system is a big deal. The Inurans build these moons like a megacity of pure commerce, stocked with everything you could imagine. Every good, delicacy, weapon, artifact or gimmick they think they can sell. They establish a trade treaty to exist in system and plan out an orbit before expending massive amounts of magical energy to teleport into the system.
527. 
528. They tend to keep to themselves otherwise and their jobs are usually odd requests and a little bit in the gray area of legality.
529. 
530. On the surface though it checks out. A merchant would be thrilled to sell old equipment back to the system when the moon lands.

540. Narrator:Tucked in a front compartment of the left suit of armor is a dragon scale pulsing a pale purple light. A dream scale. These are commonly used to encode massive amounts of information, or to power spells or magical devices, create artifacts, you name it. Information storage is the most common however.
541. 
542. As soon as you lean close though you feel a flash of something you can not quite place. Not fear, not repulsion, but colder. The metal alloy of this spell armor looks slick, like it is ever so slightly wet. It's cool to the touch if you retrieve the scale.

594. Narrator:You pull into the forward compartment and find the bridge small, dark, and suddenly not dead. Two universal magical matrices sit recessed into the deck just behind the pilot consoles: circular pads with three concentric rings suspended over each. The rings are thin, rune‑etched, and now rotating in slow, independent motion. A faint low hum rides under the movement like distant turbine blades. Soft violet and pale gray light traces the rune channels, pulsing in a staggered heartbeat sequence. Joren is slumped half across the nearer matrix pad, one knee on the ring lip, one arm dangling, helmeted head resting against the inner rotating ring. The rings drift past his shoulder without touching. His plasma cutter lies a meter away, powered down, a faint warmth still bleeding off the barrel.

Combat armor is slate gray with white unit chevrons at the left shoulder. Plates are scuffed, utilitarian, no ornament. Each marine carries a kinetic slug rifle with a heavy barrel shroud and a top strip of simple iron sights plus a short micro sensor nub. No spell focuses. Rifle stocks rest against hips or low shoulders. Three kneel at the front to keep muzzles angled upward without crossing lines. Four stand behind them in a stagger you could read like a wall. Visors are down. Status lamps at each jaw hinge show solid green. Their posture is ready but not rushed.

She is thrown against the bulkhead and gets a nasty bruise (no negative) or a cracked rib (+1 luck), her pick. 1. Narrator:Asha and Kaelith both independently took contracts that led them to the same target: a derelict Inuran stealth freighter hidden in the asteroid belt near Ternus. Asha was hired on suspiciously generous terms to tow the ship intact to dock, only to find a small salvager crew already on site. Tessa and Joren Arden along with their mage, Kaelith were determined to board. A brief standoff over salvage rights ended when Asha simply clamped on and began towing the freighter away, forcing Tessa to scramble back to her ship and leaving Kaelith and Joren to rush inside and grab whatever they could. Inside, Kaelith powered up the armory and discovered Inuran Mark V spell armor, spellpistols, stims, and an active dream dragon scale also known as an information-storing magical artifact. Joren pushed to the bridge to bring the ship online. Asha, meanwhile, flew a flawless tow back toward Ternus, called in security, and docked under the guns of Ternus marines, ready for a very official, tense handover.
2. 
3. Before security could board, everything went catastrophically wrong. On the bridge of the freighter, Joren, eyes blazing with white light, channeled a spell far beyond Kaelith's understanding through the ship’s dormant matrices. A moment later, a searing purple blast ripped through the docks and obliterated kilometers of bay. Both Asha's corvette and the freighter were thrown clear into space; Asha came away with a cracked rib and Kaelith blacked out briefly. Joren died with his eyes burned out, seemingly drained by whatever he invoked. In the chaos that followed, comms were jammed, emergency lanes lit up, and the orbital station descended into confusion. Asha chose to hold position in space rather than fight her way into a ruined dock. Kaelith, now in the strange, hungry Inuran spell armor, stabilized the freighter and began drifting away from the station. Asha moved to re‑engage; realizing the tow hardware was wrecked, she hailed the freighter and struck a deal with its lone survivor. Together they linked ships, met face‑to‑face (shotgun vs. alien war‑armor), and discovered the truth: this "simple tow" was a lost war‑era supply ship loaded with spell armor for a platoon. Spellrifles and pistols, high‑grade munitions, medical supplies, spare stealth coating, and crates of blank dream dragon scales. Enough wealth to change several lives. With the docks in ruins, their client's motives unclear, and a literal war‑treasure under their boots, Asha and Kaelith ended session one frantically loading as much of the cargo as they could onto Asha's corvette, planning to leave the freighter as the decoy "contract delivery" while they quietly vanish with a fortune.
4.

25. Emergency traffic strobes still blink in the distant dark where the Ternus docks used to be. They are a ragged gouge of shattered metal and tumbling debris slowly spreading along the station's orbit. Asha's corvette and the Inuran stealth freighter drift far from the chaos in a relatively quiet pocket of space, locked together by an old standard airlock while their drives sit on low power. Inside, the corvette's cramped cargo bays are choked with stolen crates containing dragon scales in padded racks, sleek spellrifles and pistols, sealed medkits, and one spare suit of Inuran spell armor propped like a silent extra passenger. Asha's ribs throb faintly under her body armor every time she moves; Kaelith’s alien war‑plate hums faintly, HUD flickering with unfamiliar Inuran sigils and a slow, unsettling "magic" meter that seems locked at zero. Local comms are jammed with half‑coherent distress calls and rerouted traffic orders, but for the moment no one seems to be looking directly at them. In the hush between panicked transmissions, the two of you have a stolen war cache, a disabled tow contract, a dead tech mage on a drifting freighter, and a rapidly closing window to decide: vanish with the haul, bluff your way back into the system, or try to learn what Joren actually unleashed on Ternus.

77. Asha straps into the command chair, runs a quick systems check, and severs the airlock link to the freighter with a staged depressurization. She gives the freighter a small, erratic thrust so it drifts and tumbles like a crippled, abandoned hull and kills its transponder. With cargo secured, she applies a clean, quiet burn to back both ships clear of the dock and dials down emissions to a minimal profile. She seeds a faint distress ping and scrubs identifying telemetry to sell the story of an abandoned wreck. Then she sets a low‑visibility course and cuts engines to a slow, off‑grid vector while Kaelith finishes stowing manifests.

311. Narrator:Kaelith holds out the dream scale and pushes a little bit of magic into it. A swirl of purple mist above it materializes quickly into an augury, a recorded vision of someone scrying into the future.
312. 
313. The augury unfurls like a cold proof. Two faces spin into view and you both recognize they are yourselves, the vision precise enough to feel like an exact recording. Seeing themselves replayed in this light hits like a betrayal, or awe, or any number of things. Whatever it is, their faces are here. And then the augury melds away into a view of them on their respective ships, parked at the docks. The observer pulls back and shows both of you in your ships at the docks, minutes before the blast; a searing light erupts, but it's wrong, it doesn't bleed from the freighter or its bridge but splashes out from the station itself, concentrated along walls and ceiling ribs in jagged, uneven shafts, as if the station were the thing being burned open. The observer's view stays untouched by the glare, able to watch without flinch, which makes the moment feel both rehearsed and deliberate rather than chaotic. Then the tableau folds away into a cold, humming star-map: every known catalyst in the galaxy, annotated with tiny sigils. One node in particular is traced in red outline with a path leading to the Ternus system. A single locus the augury insists is important.

Narrator:As your corvette knifes out of the purple-scarred void, New Texas swells in the viewports: a blue‑green world with broad tan continents and clean white storms, its night side pricked with sparse city lights and the hard, geometric glimmer of defense grids and ship‑killer platforms. Ahead and above, the real skyline is metal. Vast orbital shipyards and docks bristle with guns; busy tugs and lifters thread between the armored flanks of destroyers, cruisers, and battleships mustering for another wave. Below, the day side rolls into view as a patchwork of ranchland and cropland, mirror‑bright irrigation, and nearly empty highways that make the place look almost too pristine after Ternus' smog. Even from up here it feels like a frontier with teeth: most of the cities hollowed out by the last war, most of the ground given over to cattle and grain, and under all that big sky a culture that assumes every stranger is either here to work, trade, or test how much punishment their security can put out.

Narrator:Lone Star Station is a rotating wheel of steel and glass the size of a small city, bolted to the spine of New Texas' main shipyards: one side faces the planet with broad viewing galleries and shuttle berths, the other bristles with gantries, drydocks, and gun platforms where destroyers and battleships sit in various states of assembly under the watchful glare of automated batteries. Inside, the main concourse is a press of spacer bars, hiring halls, cargo brokers, and cramped bunkhouses. Hard-hatted New Texan engineers rub shoulders with Confederacy marines between contracts, grim mercenary outfits advertising "Primal experience preferred," and a whole subculture of Catalyst-chasers and relic hunters who'd sell their grandmothers for a half‑credible lead on an uncontested god‑corpse, making it the perfect place to quietly pitch "high‑risk salvage with a real shot at a Catalyst" without ever mentioning a map.

Beyond, Bay 23 opens into a low‑ceiling warehouse lit by harsh strip lights. Aisles of mismatched crates, stripped spell‑rifle racks, and pallet stacks of "agri‑tools" that are obviously refitted weapon parts, with a caged back room where more serious hardware glints behind mesh. At a cluttered central desk surrounded by holo‑ledgers and weighing scales, Maris Konn looks up from a flickering manifest, eyes flicking from Asha to Kaelith, and offers a dry half‑smile that says she’d already checked their transponder ten minutes ago.

663. Sshado C. (GM):The bar sits in a recessed corner off a quieter spur of the station. It is a wide, low room of stained metal and dark wood paneling that someone dragged up from planet-side, its air thick with smoke, sizzling fat, and the bite of good whiskey. A long bar runs one side, taps and bottles under a flickering neon steer skull, but tonight nearly every stool and table is owned by Black Trace: clusters of ex‑soldiers in mixed civvies and worn combat gear, laughing too loud over trays of brisket, ribs, and overflowing beer pitchers, their rifles and armor stacked in casual, practiced piles within arm's reach. At the back, under a hanging holo of a rust‑red New Texas sunset, a long table has been half‑cleared; Rho Anselm sits there with Mudcat, Lysa, and Tamsin, a few empty chairs left like deliberate bait, and when Asha and Kaelith step through the door Rho catches sight of them, lifts a dripping rib in salute, and waves them over with a captain's easy assumption they'll come.
664.

Narrator:Rho sits at the head of the table in a faded captain's jacket, half‑turned so he can watch the whole bar, calmly carving strips off a slab of brisket while he needles Mudcat about "losing another engine to duct tape religion," which earns him a grease‑stained middle finger and a slurred defense about "field‑tested solutions" between mouthfuls of ribs. Lysa has a glass of something high‑octane and ugly in one hand, her other hand idly tracing tiny drifting ember‑motes over the tabletop as she and Tamsin argue in low voices about whether Primals dream. Lysa insists they do, Tamsin dryly betting a week's pay that "if they do, they dream about getting shot in the head." When Rho spots Asha and Kaelith, he finishes whatever joke he was telling ("…and that's when the Titan decided the cruiser was a toothpick"), lets the laughter roll a beat, then lifts his glass and calls, "Morrow! Bring your Shayan and sit; nobody leaves this table hungry or uninformed."
676.

Narrator:Asha, the eight‑odd hours between deal and departure vanish in pieces: a hot shower that never quite washes the station smoke out of your hair, four hours of dead sleep in your bunk with the ship’s hum like a heartbeat under your ribs, then lukewarm coffee and checklists. Systems lights march down your boards in orderly green: spell drive idle‑ready, spell cannon slaved to the matrix, armor and weapons stowed exactly where your hands will want them when things go wrong. By the time Lone Star’s chrono rolls over to departure slot, your fretting over every readout has settled into something harder and simpler. This is as ready as she gets.
42. 
43. Kaelith's hours blur with the quieter commerce of war: a narrow, overbright station shop where a bored clerk slides cases of mana beer and neatly labeled counterspell vials across the counter; a brief, practical meeting with Rho's quartermaster to sync long‑range squad comm bands so Black Trace and Asha's corvette can talk without the station listening in. Sleep comes in a few shallow hours on the bunk they've given you, spell armor standing in the corner like a silent, waiting animal, its HUD faintly pulsing as you nudge at its systems and learn its hungers; by the time you're back on the bridge, helmet sealed, it feels less like borrowed relic and more like an extension of your own intent.

255. Realspace slams back in like a held breath, and for a long second the only thing in the viewports is black, honest vacuum, not the infinite abyss of the Umbral Depths. Then the stars resolve and the shadow fills in around you: a cold, airless planet hanging below like a dark coin, its night side swallowing starlight, and beyond it a wide band of stone and ice arcing across the system, an asteroid belt so broad it looks like someone hammered a scar through the sky. Your corvette and Black Trace’s ship drift together inside the planet’s umbral cone, drives idling low; HUDs bloom with fresh sensor returns, static still crackling from the jump, while somewhere out there, deeper in the belt, something tugs at trajectories just enough to register as wrong. Rocks drift a half‑degree off expected, a faint smear of dream-magic in the background band like someone whispering through the static.

Asha is there, so are Black Trace, at least some of them. Behind him something swarms, ahead of him is the gap between the mandible of Aashitak's jaw and the spine at the back of what was once her throat. The frozen flesh barely resembles anything organic, and the bone protudes through it many places where it has either been eaten or eroded away. Part of the spine has broken away, revealing a light within it that draws Kaelith in, calling to him, but it's too far away. Asha will never make it.

Narrator:As the improvised cover grows into a looming wall that fills the view, she rides its motion with small, precise bursts of thrust, bleeding speed just enough that, at the last possible moment before the asteroid slams past the socket's rim, she kicks the corvette sideways and down. The hull skims the edge of Aashitak's fractured brow like a thrown blade, then drops out of the rock's shadow and into the deeper dark of the open mouth below, Asha threading the gap between two jagged teeth of bone with centimeters to spare and leaving the sacrificial asteroid to plow on ahead as the only obvious thing anyone, or anything, should have noticed.
486. 
487. The first wisps come in like fog. Their forms are pale, eel-slick strands of light that drift up from Aashitak's bones and coil around the corvette''s hull, smearing across the viewports as if something on the other side of the glass is trying to remember your shape. Asha watches her boards flicker as readings multiply: false proximity alarms, phantom engine temps, a dozen contradictory nav pings that feel less like bad sensors and more like something gently pushing every number out of tune. Deep in the drive readouts, timing markers begin to stutter a fraction of a second off-beat, threatening a misfire that would kick the ship sideways into the densest part of the wisp swarm.

505. Narrator:She has no room to kill thrust, so she flies through it. Asha's hands are light on the controls as she ignores the lying overlays and flies by the few instruments that still ring true, trimming power with quick manual overrides. Outside, the Dream-primals thicken into half-formed shapes that snap at the corvette''s wake and leave trails of cold static across the hull, but Asha rides the jitter in the drive like turbulence, rolling the ship between drifting knots of light and letting the worst of it slough off against Aashitak's own bone.

545. Narrator:The deeper the corvette slides into Aashitak's jaw, the more the space ahead looks like an exposed landscape instead of a skull. Miles of off-white bone ridges and valleys, knotted with old fractures and frozen flesh, glazed in dream glass and puddled black globs of decayed marrow that reflect false constellations. Out on the broader planes and higher ridges she can see the movement now: humanoid eidolons walking patrol-like arcs, hound-shaped Nightmares loping on too many limbs, and loose skeins of wisps streaming between splinters of black crystals like they are following some silent weather pattern in the dreamfield. But the surface is so vast that it is easy to disappear in the small places, and Asha finally picks out a shallow valley between two fused ridges where bone has sagged and cracked into a dish-shaped hollow, a pocket of relative quiet hidden under the shadow of a jutting spur.
546. 
547. Any closer and she risks alerting the natives to them, but it's going to be a hard, twenty minute sprint on foot at best.

Narrator:Assha coasts the last stretch on minimal thrust, letting the ship settle in low behind a jag of broken marrow so that from above they are just another dark gap in the godflesh, power levels dropping one by one until only life support and a thin skein of wards hum in the background. From the bridge, the view out is a tilted slice of Aashitak's skin-plain crawling with distant primals, but down here in the groove the noise of the dreamfield feels muted, like the wake of the Half-Slumbering cannot quite reach into this crack, and for the first time since they arrived the corvette feels small enough to be truly hidden.
568. 
569. Rho brings Black Trace in low and dirty, tucking his scarred gunship into the same sagging valley on the far side of Aashitaks bone ridge so that, from above, the two ships read like nothing more than a pair of shadows in a world made of scars. When his landing struts bite into fossilized marrow, the side ramp slams down and twenty mercs spill out in disciplined fireteams, armor scuffed and guns hot, fanning into a tight perimeter while Mudcat lopes off to run a quick radiation and atmosphere sweep, Lysa closes her eyes to listen for anything paying them too much attention, and Tamsin ghost-walks up the nearest rise to set a long gun where she can see both the approach to the Catalyst and anything that might come sniffing at their hides.

593. OOC - Good opportunity to reminder to use the cheat sheets and also bear with me as we figure out combat. Conceptually it's quite easy. Standard initiative system. You get two actions which can be anything, double movement, double attack, move and attack, etc...
594. 
595. OOC - Generally it's Brace for melee defense, Dodge for ranged, and Skepticism for magic. Accuracy adds a die to the roll. Double range on weapons is +2 DT. Movement is Agility _2 in meters. Sprint is agility_ 5 but uses both actions.
596. 
597. And Rolling to Hit is easy. For example, if Asha has 3 agility and 4 ranged, she rolls 7 dice to to shoot a rifle. If she meets or exceeds opponent's defense then the attaack hits. Damage is base damage + 1 for each extra success on the hit.
598. 
599. OOC: Likewise, Kaelith with a spell rifle would use literally the same dice. Agility and Ranged, since it's a gun.

655. Narrator:The climb to the ridge is short but ugly, boots crunching over frost-stiff bone and old flesh, breath loud in helmets as something presses faintly at the edges of thought. When Asha, Rho, and the point elements of Black Trace crest the lip and go prone beside Tamsin, the view over the far side steals what is left of their idle conversation: a vast, sloping plain of Aashitak''s inner jaw running down toward the broken spine, the distance between here and there a hard twenty-minute sprint even at full push in low gravity. Far ahead, the severed vertebrae rise like shattered white pillars as large as entire cities out of a slow river of crystallized pink-blood and ice, void Shards jutting from the wound in a ragged, black crown that drinks the light and leaks it back in strangely colored shadows.
656. 
657. Between the ridge and that impossible landmark, the plain crawls. Humanoid eidolons patrol in loose ranks along invisible lines of force, some wearing bone masks, some half-faded at the edges like they cannot quite decide to be real; packs of Nightmare hounds pace wider, circling and re-circling scent trails that never quite lead them to the valley where the ships lie hidden. Wisps stream overhead in curling ribbons, riding currents only they can feel, occasionally dipping low to brush against patches of strange resin that glisten like a purple oil in old impact craters. There are gaps: faintly quieter channels between patrol routes, shallow depressions and jagged bone ridges that sketch a broken, indirect path toward the spine, just barely wide enough for a small, fast team willing to trust in speed, shadows, and whatever cover a dead god' jaw will give them.
658. 
659. Asha Morrow:Asha follows that indirect path with her eyes considering it quietly, options are thin here. She points it out and traces a path, "This might be our way forward. Do you see there? If we follow the gaps between patrols we have a chance without drawing too much attention."

You're practically impossible to catch if you try, you could abandon these folk and be there before they even make it five hundred meters.

877. Narrator:You know _where_ you are supposed to be, Kaelith, but the magic here is overwhelming your senses. Everything is steeped in syrup-thick dream magic that you cannot see through. Asha, you can discern that you're definitely in a jungle, definitely not where you should be.

895. Narrator:Asha keeps her head down and pushes, picking a direction that feels like "forward" and driving her legs through the tangle without giving the jungle timeto argue. Vines snag at her greaves, branches slap against her visor, insects rattle on her armor in sheets, but she forces a rhythm out of it: step, shove, breathe, repeat, refusing to let the howls and the wrongness in the air slow her. After what feels like several minutes of hard slog the ground tilts under her boots, the thin stream now running at her feet in the same direction she is going instead of across her path, and for a disorienting heartbeat she has the sick certainty that she has been walking in circles around a single, unseen point that is watching to see how long she will keep playing along.

Narrator:Kaelith's sigils burn into his vision, and the jungle peels away like wet paper; with Piercing Gaze pledged, the syrup-thick dreamfield thins and then shatters, and in the next blink he is back on Aashitak's jaw, boots on bone, low gravity tugging exactly the way it should. The plain snaps into brutal clarity: Black Trace spread out along the indirect path mid-sprint, some of them stumbling or firing wildly at things only they can see, two mercs down on one knee clutching at invisible wounds while wisps cling to their helmets and shoulders like threads of pale fungus.
916. 
917. Ahead, Asha is running hard and straight, shotgun low, pace perfect but eyes unfocused, her stride just slightly out of sync with the real terrain as if she is following a path only the dream wants her to see. Overhead, Kaelith can now see the true shapes of the primals riding them: filaments of Dreamlight netted between wisps and eidolons out on the flanks, all of it tugging at his comrades' senses, trying to drag them sideways into pocket jungles and buried memories. With Piercing Gaze active, those lines stand out like glowing tripwires against the stark white of Aashitak's bone, and Kaelith is suddenly the only one here who can see both the trap and the way through it.

1025. Narrator:The Lacuna shard that nearly took Kaelith's head is not alone. As it finishes skidding and digs to a halt in the godflesh, a second and third black crystal the size of dropships slam into the jaw further out, each impact sending a teeth-rattling vibration through the bone and kicking up plumes of powdered bone that hang in the air like ghostly fire. For a heartbeat it looks random, like debris falling from some unseen orbit, until Kaelith's sharpened sight catches the pattern: the shards are not falling, they are being thrown.
1026. 
1027. Far above the ragged crown of Lacuna Shards that seal Aashitak's sundered spine, something impossibly vast uncoils: a wyrm of splintered bone ribs and flesh seething with pink colored light, its body threading in and out of reality as it wraps around the shard ring. Chunks of entropic bone grind against crystal with a sound like glaciers breaking, and every time one of its coils passes through the physical the air around the team thickens, colors oversaturate, and for a moment they see themselves from outside, like pieces on a board the Guardian is studying. One titanic skull, half-formed and mask-like, turns just enough that empty sockets line up with the little knot of figures by the Catalyst, and a surge of cold, focused awareness slams through the dream, pinning every mortal mind there with the crushing certainty that The Half-Slumbering is no longer content to just dream of them.
1028.

1034. 
1035. Narrator:The crack in the spine ahead is utterly massive. The hole itself might as well be a great lake, and within it a forest of impossible vast cables like thick redwood trees made of glass making up each nerve fiber of Aashitak. They glow with a syrupy pink light which threatens to overwhelm you, yet somehow you can see through it at the same time.
1036. 
1037. You can choose to stay and fight or run.
1038. 
1039. Asha Morrow:Asha turns and hits a dead sprint towards the catalyst, saying nothing as the adrenaline fueled tunnel vision hits.

1044. 
1045. 
1046. 
1047. Narrator:As you pass through the crack into the spinal column you pass out of conscious reality. An infinite pool of pink light stretches out before you, around you, through every atom in every cell of your body. An ocean of power within a mind so vast that you are not even a drop. You become that consciousness and the universe stretches out before you through thousands of senses you can not comprehend.
1048. 
1049. You are old, ancient enough to understand how the world was created. You were there to see the very first strands of the realm of Dream woven into existence using magic. You understand the eight aspects and the paths which can be accessed by combining them. The complexities behind true magic seem trivial. You were there when reality was created. One of the oldest Gods, not a Titan, but close.
1050. 
1051. The power is endless. You could create a world, an entire species, or extinguish one. You have already done so before; you watched as your children were hunted and extinguished by many younger Gods.
1052. 
1053. One in particular fades into memory, and suddenly you realize you have a body. You _are_ Aashitak, curled up in the realm of Dream, surrounded by those who would one day become your children. You, Aashitak, distinctly feel the sensation of being watched by two of your own children. You are staring down at both of them and they stare back. Asha and Kaelith stare at themselves through the eyes of Aashitak.
1054. 
1055. Then suddenly the dream is torn. A vast seam of void erupts around Aashitak. Through the tear, two vast arms like rings of a planet reach through and wrap themselves around Aashitak, pulling her down, through reality, into the Umbral Depths.
1056. 
1057. "WHERE... IS... ETERRA?" screams your brother, Zenzuata.
1058. 
1059. 
1060. 
1061. Narrator:"Zen, you fool," Aashitak says. "She's gone."
1062. 
1063. The void around you shudders as Zenzuata’s grip tightens, his ringed arms grinding against the fabric of Dream, and fissures of nothing begin to spiderweb out from where he holds you. For a heartbeat that could be an eon, you feel three perspectives at once: Aashitak’s ancient sorrow, Asha’s vertigo as her own eyes gaze back at her from her creator’s face, and Kaelith’s cold, analytical awareness mapping every fracture of the Dream as if it were a battlefield about to be lost. In that overlapping instant of selves, you understand that Zenzuata is not merely tearing at you, but at reality itself, each crack in reality reaching backwards, searching for every thread where she ever was so that he can unmake the answer to his question.
1064. 
1065. "If you do this, Talifax won't stop with just _killing you_. Don't you see he has deceived us? Nobody will be able to oppose him, Zen. Eterra chose to die so we COULD fight!"
1066. 
1067. All Zenzuata can do is scream. A deep sense of pain and grief overwhelms you. Gods can't truly die, not the same way mortals can. They can be shattered and their pieces scattered across the cosmos, but they will always try to pull themselves back together. The overwhelming loss you feel is the feeling of your being torn back across space and time, erased, pushed into the void. In a moment of desperation you unleash everything, dumping your power and freezing yourself into perpetual dream, forcefully locking your body across multiple domains. Your head in the dream, your chest in reality, and the rest forever being dragged into the umbral depths by Zenzuata.
1068. 
1069. As you fade from the memories of Aashitak, you remember her conversations with the keeper of secrets. You recall conversations with Reveanthara and Xal about The Great Cycle. The endless whispers in the libraries of someone who cannot be named woven by his arachne librarian seers. The secrets of the universe unfold like plain logic and conversation. You feel the pull to fall deeper into it. (DT 3 Skepticism Check)
1070.

1199. Narrator:Asha, you find yourself _affixed_ to the nearest trunk of crystal nerve fiber. It has formed some kind of a crust against your armor on the gauntlets, arms, anywhere that was in contact. It's growing as you stare at it, but it's not strong enough to hold you here yet.

1217. Narrator:Asha, as you pull away you feel an overwhelming sense of power rush through you for the first time in your life. Every sense is alight with raw power. You don't just feel strong, you feel _great_, like you could forget what hunger and pain were. You can taste the magic in the air, feel your hands move through it like a thick syrup. You can push on it and tug at it, and it moves through you. Within that energy you feel Aashitak's mind pulsing quietly, silent but there. Not part of your soul, but part of the magic it moves. A shape imprinted onto your own, that of a god. Within it an endless ocean of knowledge just out of your reach, and with that comes a certain draw to drink more deeply of the power here. But you know you still have a mission to do.
1218. 
1219. Kaelith, you are suffused with raw dream magic. For the first time in your life you see the literal flows of magic in the world around you. Asha does not know how rare this is, an unheard-of gift. The entire world is magic. You can see at least void, fire, and dream swirling around you. With them are many other colors but not ones you can properly perceive yet. You can only presume they are the other elements.
1220.

When Essence Sight snaps into focus, the Half-Slumbering stops being a monster and becomes a wound in the world: a vast, tangled knot of Dream-light coiled around the shattered spine, every scale and rib a conduit where Aashitak’s remaining power is being siphoned and rerouted instead of radiating freely. The wyrm’s body is threaded through with blinding currents that run out into the eidolons, hounds, and wisps, each primal lit like a bulb on a circuit it controls, while great ropes of that same light dive back into the Guardian. Where its gaze passes over Asha and Kaelith, she can see the flows stutter and split around the Inuran armor on his skin, the Guardian’s Dream trying and failing to run cleanly through something in him that drinks a different kind of power, leaving a jagged, hungry void in the pattern that even a dead god’s usurper cannot quite read.

15. Narrator:Lone Star's lights are long behind you.
16. 
17. You sold a war-freighter haul, made Maris disgustingly rich, synced comm bands with Black Trace, and slipped into the Umbral Depths under Kaelith's sigils, two ships running dark in the planet's shadow. Out here, Aashitak's corpse is both a landmark and a weather system. Dreamglass dust, false sensor returns, wisps that "remember" you wrong. You rode an asteroid into a dead god's mouth, parked as close as you dared, and ran.
18. 
19. Then the Dream caught you.
20. 
21. It split the team into pocket realities, jungles, loops, wrong directions, until Kaelith forced clarity with Piercing Gaze and physically ripped Asha and Lysa free, flying them through the tripwires toward the Catalyst embedded in the broken spine. Inside the nerve column, you drowned in Aashitak's last memory: gods screaming, void tearing Dream, a corpse that refuses to be only dead. You pulled back... but not unchanged.
22. 
23. **You catalyzed.** Dream magic took root. **Essence Sight** opened. And when you stepped back into realspace, the Guardian was waiting, wearing Aashitak's power like stolen skin and calling itself The Half-Slumbering. It tailed you like you were prey it wanted to savor. Below, Black Trace died in pieces. You grabbed Rho. You tried to save Tamsin. And the wyrm's attention kept snagging on one thing: Kaelith's Inuran spell armor and a jagged "hunger" in the flow it couldn't read.
24. 
25. Now, **right now**, you're at your ships, surrounded.
26. 
27. 
28. 
29. Narrator:Kaelith: your suit hits 100% and you trigger the fully-charged function. You rocket up and **detonate into black tendrils**, a web of void-like filaments that lash out and attach to primals across hundreds of meters. Eidolons. Hounds. Wisps. Their Dreamlight screams as it's siphoned, channeled, drunk, dragged into your armor so fast the world near you loses color. Your HUD floods with a target count in the **hundreds**.
30. 
31. Asha: Essence Sight snaps on and the Guardian stops being a monster and becomes a circuit, Aashitak's remaining Dream rerouted through it, primals lit like bulbs on its grid. What you see is beautiful and peaceful, but wounded. The primals fear you, the Guardian too. When its gaze passes Kaelith, the flow of magic from the catalyst stutters and tears around that armor like water hitting a blade.
32. 
33. Then the Half-Slumbering moves.
34. 
35. It drops behind your corvette, plants a clawed foot on the hull, and, screaming in rage, **crushes your ship like a tin can.** Metal screams. Glass and plating fold. There is no sound in low gravity, but you can feel it in your bones if you're on the surface of the catalyst.
36. 
37. Above you, Kaelith, the siphoned power becomes a pressure behind your eyes and a weight in your chest: **you have a beam to fire**, something that feels made to kill gods. You already chose the only safe direction you could think of: **into the nothingness of space.**
38. 
39. The moment before discharge hangs like a held breath.
40. 
41. Narrator: Kaelith:The beam is about to go. What does "firing it into space" look like, tight, controlled lance; wide, venting spray; sustained burn or a single pulse? And what do you do to keep the suit from pulling more than you can bleed off?
42. 
43. Narrator: Asha:Your ship is collapsing under a god's foot. Where are you in relation to it **this second**, and what do you do to avoid getting pinned, shredded, or thrown when the beam hits and the Dreamfield reacts?
44.

The light isn't light, it's absence shaped into a weapon, and as it blooms it drinks the color out of the sky around it, a long solid blast that does not flicker.

601. For the first time in minutes, the sound in your helmets is mostly your own breathing and the ship's fans. It smells like hot metal, old gun oil, and blood in a filter. The corridor lights are down in red, emergency low. Rho's ship shudders once, not an impact so much as the godflesh outside remembering you exist.
602. 
603. Behind you, the ramp motor whines as it locks. Somewhere in the hull, something ticks like a cooling barrel. The air inside is thin but real. It has edges. It feels like shelter.

61. Narrator:In the quiet after escape, the ship rides dark in the Umbral Depths and the adrenaline finally drains out, leaving an awful emptiness where Black Trace's voices should be. Asha rips off her helmet, raw and shaking, trying to process that her ship is gone; Rho stays in captain mode and does the impossible anyway, keys the intercom, calls for status checks, and lets the silence answer, while Lysa keeps a hand on the spell matrix like it's the only solid thing left, murmuring that "Aashitak chose" and tasting the Catalyst in the back of her throat. Kaelith, still rattled by how hungry his Inuran armor felt, cannot stop replaying the Guardian's warning and starts scanning the suit for what it really is. And then, just for a breath, the pressure breaks as talk turns practical, Lone Star as a stop, New Texas shipyards for a replacement, and the five of them alive, bruised, and bonded fall into gallows humor and tentative plans about what kind of ship they'll buy and what it would mean to become a real crew.
62. 
63. The trip through the Umbral Depths is short and somber, interrupted by a return to real space in the shadow of the planet. New Texas swells big and blue-green beyond the glass, all clean storms and tan continents, but the real skyline is metal: vast orbital shipyards and docks bristling with guns, tugs and lifters threading between armored hulls like insects around a carcass. Asha guides the ship into the dockyards, performing the usual song and dance with docking control.
64. 
65. You made it back alive. You also made it back changed. And whatever you brought out of Aashitak feels quieter here, but not gone.
66. 
67. Narrator: Asha:Asha, your comm pings into Lone Star's local mesh, ad walls and shipbroker feeds sliding into place like they were waiting for you to blink. The public listings for ships are all teeth, but small teeth: corvettes with fresh paint and overstated kill counts, "light escorts" that are just civilian hulls with a gun bolted on and a prayer written inside the paneling. Anything bigger reads as "military contract only" or "yard allocation pending" or just does not exist on the open boards. Plenty of spell fighters but that would only be a sidegrade at best.

73. Narrator: Kaelith:Kaelith, you get a terminal and a quiet corner, and for a moment the station noise becomes a metronome. You pull up old records, half-sanitized war summaries, ship design notes, and the kind of forums where veterans argue about names they still can't say out loud without spitting.
74. 
75. The word that keeps resurfacing is the one the Half-Slumbering threw at you like a curse: "Nefarious."
76. 
77. You find fragments first, then a shape. Inuran shipwrights sold a class of design that's not just armored, but hungry: ships built to consume mana and turn it back against wyrms and spellships. A weapon concept dressed up as a hull. In the old war, Ternus wanted a way to break Shaya for what they believed was Shaya's role in the destruction of Colony 3, and they bought the lie that this new weapon would do it.
78. 
79. The lie had a name, if you read between the missing pages. Nefarious. The story ends in a way no admiral report likes to admit: the deception culminated in catastrophe. The entire fleet was consumed as raw material to form a new body built from ships and souls and stolen magic. The response was not one faction but it was everyone who still had a reason to keep the universe intact. Names recur in the surviving accounts: Aaron and Nara, Guardians of Xal; Voria of Shaya; Frit, Guardian of Nebiat; Cruz of Yanthara; the God Inura; the wyrms of Virkon. All turned on Nefarious to put her down. What remained of the war supplies was destroyed, on purpose, because nobody trusted themselves with it.

191. Narrator: Kaelith:Lone Star's dock ring hits you like a wall of noise and routine. The air smells like coolant, hot metal, and fried food. New Texas is on war footing up close with security drones driting in lazy but armed arcs. Yard workers move in coordinated waves, and every other bulkhead screen is a muster notice, a casualty ticker, or an ad for "priority refits". You catch the usual stares the moment you step out because you are Shayan. A curiosity. Ahead, the shipbroker kiosks and the shipyard access corridors are busy, tight with captains arguing allocations, and every path forward feels like it runs through the same three things: contracts, favors, or stupid people looking to make money.
